# Enrang testing

Part I is the user-facing `enrang test` runner. Part II is the compiler's own test strategy. Language behaviour is in `SPEC.md` section 12; this document adds execution mechanics and everything the toolchain tests about itself.

**Status: M0's tests exist and pass, and later layers do not exist yet.** Items marked [measured] were run on the development host on 2026-11-05 (i5-12400F, WSL2, `as` 2.42, rustc 1.97.1). All other numbers are targets, and the counts in section 11 are for M10. The compiler and `enrang dev` run only on a modern x86-64 machine; only their output runs on old chips.

# Part I: `enrang test`

## 1. API

SPEC 12 is normative. A test file:

```ts
// src/stack.test.en: an extra part of module src/stack.en, so its private items are visible
private function twoUsers(): Stack<User> {  // a fixture is a function; teardown is the destructor
  let s = new Stack<User>();
  s.push(new User { name: "Ada", age: 36 });
  s.push(new User { name: "Grace", age: 85 });
  return s;
}

describe("Stack", () => {
  test("pop is LIFO", () => {
    let s = twoUsers();
    expect(s.pop()).toBe(new User { name: "Grace", age: 85 });
  });

  test.panics("bad index", "out of bounds", () => {
    const xs: i64[] = [];
    console.log(xs[0]);
  });

  test("heavy", () => { sortMillion(); }, { fuel: 2000000000, memory: "64MB" });
  test.todo("handles unicode");
});
```

Forms: `describe`, `test`, `test.only`, `test.skip`, `test.todo`, `test.panics(name, substring, body)`, `test.each(name, cases, body)`, `compileFails`, `compileOk`, and `expect(a).matcher(b);` as a statement. Options are a literal `{ fuel, memory, allow }`. `beforeEach` and friends get EN1105 with a helper-function fix-it. A `test` declared inside a function is EN1110. A test arrow returns `void` unless annotated `(): Result<void> => ...` or `(): Result<void, E> => ...`, and `?` and `return` act on the arrow.

## 2. Why compiler forms, and how they lower

An ordinary library `expect` cannot exist: by-value it would move its operand, by reference it would need a `&` at every call, and `enrang test` runs no top-level statements, so nothing could register tests at run time.

- **`test` and `describe` are items.** They live only in `*.test.en` files, the test part of a module; in any other file they are EN1112, with a fix-it that moves them. Names are string literals, so IDs (`A > B > c`) are stable and filtering happens before anything runs. Duplicates are EN1109. In `ParseMode::Build` test parts are left out, so a module builds byte-identical with or without its `.test.en` file (meta-test).
- **A test body** becomes an ordinary function; `mirbuild` lowers it like any other. The driver adds a generated `__test_table` static of `{ id, body, flags, fuel, memory, panic_substring, cases: Option<FnRef> }`.
- **`test.each`** sets `cases` to its generator. The case count is known only at run time, so case IDs such as `parses[2]`, zero-based, are emitted at run time. `-t` filters on the row ID first and on the case index when the generator has run. Fuel and leak balance are per case. Natively the generator is charged and balanced once per process start, and in the VM each case gets a fresh heap and regenerates. The generator must be deterministic. The final `Tests` line counts cases. The body's parameter is read-only, as for every role the compiler calls: a body that writes it is EN0615.
- **`expect(a).toBe(b);`** lowers to `t1 = &a; t2 = &b; r = EqGlue<T>(t1, t2); if (!r) __fail(site, ShowGlue(t1), ShowGlue(t2));`. These are real shared borrows for one statement, so a live exclusive borrow still gets EN0708. Rvalues become temporaries dropped at statement end.
- **`__eq<T>` (`EqGlue`)** is compiler-derived deep structural equality. A user's `Eq` is never consulted, so code under test does not grade itself. Floats follow `Object.is`: `+0` differs from `-0`, and `NaN` equals `NaN`.
- **`__show<T>` (`ShowGlue`)** is generated MIR that emits tagged records on the channel of section 5. It never allocates, and it sends a float as its bits for the parent to format, while a value whose type writes `fmt` goes as the text that `fmt` writes, which the binary runs only once the matcher has failed, with the formatter it carries for T0007 (section 5). Ownership makes values a tree, and the reference fields of borrowing classes (SPEC 5.1) cannot close a cycle, so the walk terminates without cycle detection. Raw pointers print as addresses and are never followed.
- **Peeling.** Matchers peel one `&` and one `| null` from the actual type; R2 caps the depth. Literals adapt to the inner type.
- **Test-only code.** `__eq` and `__show` exist only in test builds.

With no unwinding, a failed `expect` ends the test and no drops run. The VM discards the heap; a native process dies and is resumed at the next test by the parent. A panic cannot be caught inside a test, so `test.panics` is a test-level verdict and `toThrow` does not exist.

## 3. Matchers

All accept `.not`. Unknown matcher is EN1108.

| Group | Matchers |
|---|---|
| Equality | `toBe(v)` |
| Bool | `toBeTrue()`, `toBeFalse()` |
| Null, Result | `toBeNull()`, `toBeOk(v?)`, `toBeErr(v?)` |
| Enums | `toMatchPattern(ArmValue)`, lowered to `match` |
| Order | `toBeGreaterThan`, `toBeGreaterThanOrEqual`, `toBeLessThan`, `toBeLessThanOrEqual` |
| Floats | `toBeCloseTo(v, eps)`, `toBeNaN()`, `toHaveBits(bits)` |
| Containers, strings | `toHaveLength(n)`, `toContain(x)`, `toStartWith(s)`, `toEndWith(s)` |
| Snapshots | `toMatchSnapshot()`, `toMatchInlineSnapshot(raw`...`)` |

`toBeErr(v)` compares a `Failure` by its text (`toBeErr("negative age")`) and an enum error by its case (`toBeErr(AgeError.Negative)`). `toMatchPattern` takes the values one `match` arm lists, such as `Shape.Circle`, and binds nothing.

Jest arrivals get EN1104: `toEqual` and `toStrictEqual` get a rename fix-it to `toBe`; `toBeTruthy` explains there is no truthiness; `toThrow` points to `test.panics`; `toHaveBeenCalled` suggests injecting through `T extends Clock`.

## 4. Execution model

```text
enrang test [paths]
  -t "Stack > pop"           substring plus `*`, on test IDs
  --only-failed  --bail      failures recorded in out/.last-failed
  --target=<t> --cpu=<l> --float=<f>   run natively
  --matrix                   every runnable cell for the host
  --layout=vm64|vm32  --oracle  --fuel=<n>   VM options
  --isolate=test             respawn per test (native)
  --shuffle=<seed>           order, for flushing out mutable statics
  --strict                   native-only tests fail in the VM (EN1201)
  --reporter=pretty|dots|json|junit
  -u                         update snapshots
  --ci                       refuse test.only (EN1106), never create snapshots, no timings, print effective grants
  --allow-read=<paths>  --allow-write=<paths>   command-line grants; the only way to grant outside the project root
  --watch                    mtime poller shared with enrang dev; reruns tests whose reachable MIR changed
  --jobs=<n>
```

`--coverage` is deferred (EN1041). The VM's block counters stay, for the meta-tests of section 18.

**VM, the default.** One process, one worker thread per core. The front end runs once per file and MIR is shared by `Arc`. Each test gets a fresh heap and globals image. A panic returns to the worker and the heap is discarded. VM frames live on the heap, so stack overflow is a panic.

**Native, on request.** Each test file becomes one binary with a static test table. The parent runs one binary per core, starting it with `--enrang-test-start=N:K`, row `N`, case `K` (0 for an ordinary test); the binary continues until it exits. A panic or timeout kills only that process, and the parent resumes at the next test or case, re-running the generator for a `test.each` row. Per-test spawn is affordable on Linux at 0.51 ms [measured] but not through WSL interop at 178 ms [measured], so this one mechanism serves all targets. `--isolate=test` forces a respawn per test. Mutable statics need `unsafe`, so a shared process only endangers `unsafe` tests, and `--shuffle` exposes those.

**Timeouts.** One fuel unit is one MIR statement or terminator. Default 200 million per test; running out is T0004. There is no wall clock in the VM, so timeouts never flake. Native uses a 10 s wall-clock watchdog in the parent (T0008).

**Isolation.** VM tests get no capabilities, a virtual clock and a per-test temp directory; `{ allow: ["read:fixtures/"] }` grants more, but only inside the project root, after canonicalisation and with `..` rejected (EN1111). So running `enrang test` on an untrusted checkout cannot read `~/.ssh` through a test's own options; anything wider needs a command-line grant. Under `--ci` the runner prints the effective grants. Natively nothing is enforced, and the runner says so once. Tests reaching `asm` or port I/O through the mono call graph are `native-only`: the VM reports the site and counts them separately.

**Verdict codes** are SPEC 12.4's T0001-T0008. Exit status 1 if anything failed, 0 otherwise.

## 5. The framed channel

Test binaries report to the parent on fd 3 on Linux, on stderr on Windows (interop gives no spare handle), on COM1 when freestanding, and through a host call in the VM. The Rust parent parses, renders and diffs, and printing never allocates (SPEC 4.4), so reporting cannot disturb allocation counts. A diff renders each value by SPEC 4.4's rule for a value inside another, at every level, a string at the top level included, because a value in a diff is never one printed on its own: it quotes a string and every text value that rule quotes, so `toBe("hi Ada")` against `"hi Ada "` shows `'hi Ada'` and `'hi Ada '`, and the trailing space is visible (`V-ME-10-diff`, section 18), and it shows a value whose type writes `fmt` through that `fmt`, unquoted, as that rule prints it, the test binary running the `fmt` once the matcher has failed, when the test's allocation counts no longer matter, and sending its text in a `T` record. A test that returns `Err` prints the error itself, `error: <e>` through its `Display`, exactly as `main` does, and sends that text to the parent as the failure text of T0007, the `text` of its `Fail` frame, which is empty for every other code, so an error whose type writes `fmt` shows its own text (`V-ME-10-err`). Test binaries, freestanding ones included, therefore carry the formatter code, which costs size but no allocation.

```text
frame:   1E 'E' 'N' kind(u8) len(u32 LE) payload
kinds:   1 TestStart(id, case)   2 TestPass(live_blocks, live_bytes)   3 Fail(code, site, text)
         4 Value(role, records)   5 PanicSeen(substring match)   6 FileEnd
```

`__show` records are tag-length-value: `B` bool, `I` width and value, `F` width and bits, `S` bytes, `R` class ID with fields, `E` enum ID and variant with fields, `A` count, `N` null, `P` raw address, `T` the text a written `fmt` wrote, shown unquoted, `C` close. Class, field and variant names come from IDs into the parent's compile data, so the `__show` records carry no name strings; the binary carries only the names its trace table and its `Display` code need, the latter for the `error: <e>` of T0007 and the text of `T` records. On Windows the parent demultiplexes frames out of stderr by the 3-byte prefix and passes the rest through as the test's stderr.

## 6. Leak detection

There is no libc and so no sanitizer; the allocator counts instead.

- **VM:** every allocation is tracked with its site.
- **Native:** test builds of `alloc` keep `live_blocks` and `live_bytes`. The runner compares them around each test through `TestPass`.
- **Rule:** a passing test must balance, or it fails with T0003, naming the allocation site in the VM. Failed tests are exempt, since no drops ran.
- **Blame:** a leak in a test reaching no user `unsafe` is labelled a compiler bug.
- `mem.forget` is safe, so a test leaking through it deliberately fails T0003 and must free or use `test.skip`.

## 7. Compile-error tests

```ts
compileFails(
  "use after move",
  raw`
  function take(u: U): void {}
  const u = new U { name: "Ada" };
  take(u);
  console.log(u.name); //~ EN0601
  private class U { public name: string; }
`,
);

compileFails(
  "bswap above the floor",
  raw`
  //! target: i386-none, cpu: i386
  private function f(): void { unsafe { asm("bswap eax"); } } //~ EN0901
`,
);

compileOk("borrow ends at last use", raw`...`);
```

The embedded compiler runs these in the runner at discovery, identically for any test target.

- The set of (line, code) pairs must match exactly, so a snippet cannot pass because of an unrelated typo. The set includes warnings. `compileFails` needs at least one anchor on an `E` code. `compileOk` may carry anchors, only for `W` codes, which is how EN0314, EN0430, EN0453, EN0503 and EN0828 fire in a program that compiles. Any mismatch is EN1107.
- `//~ EN0601 "substring"` also checks the message.
- `//!` sets `target`, `cpu`, `float`.
- `{ snapshot: true }` stores the rendered diagnostic. `{ fixes: true }` applies machine-applicable fix-its and requires the result to compile; warnings in the fixed program are accepted.
- Spans map back to the outer file through `FileOrigin::Snippet`.
- Snippets containing backticks live in `name.fail.en` beside the test file with the same anchors, and are discovered automatically. `enrang fmt` skips these files and never changes a `raw` snippet, because their anchors must stay on exact lines.

## 8. Snapshots and reporters

**Snapshots.** The parent writes them to `__snapshots__/` beside the test file, keyed by test ID and ordinal. Mismatch is T0006. `-u` updates; `--ci` never creates them.

**Streams.** All reporter output, including `fail[...]` blocks, goes to stdout. Compile diagnostics go to stderr.

| Reporter | Format |
|---|---|
| `pretty` | Section 9. `PASS file` or `FAIL file`, indented IDs, final `Tests` line. SPEC S10 is a golden |
| `dots` | One character per test, failures after |
| `json` | Versioned NDJSON, one object per line |
| `junit` | `<testsuite>` per file, `<failure type="T0001">` with the rendered text |

NDJSON:

```json
{"v":1,"event":"run_start","target":"vm64","files":1}
{"v":1,"event":"test","id":"Stack > pop is LIFO","file":"src/stack.test.en","status":"fail","code":"T0001","fuel":1204,"cell":"vm64"}
{"v":1,"event":"run_end","passed":1,"failed":1,"skipped":0,"native_only":0,"total":2}
```

## 9. A real failing test

This is the specified output for these exact files, and it becomes a golden test at M4. Only the fuel figure is illustrative until M4 blesses the real count, which is deterministic.

`src/stack.en`:

```ts
public class User {
  public name: string;
  public age: i64;
}

public class Stack<T> {
  private items: T[] = [];

  public push(x: T): void { this.items.push(x); }
  public pop(): T | null { return this.items.pop(); }
  public top(): &T | null { return this.items.last(); }
  public size(): isize { return this.items.length(); }
}
```

`src/stack.test.en`:

```ts
private function twoUsers(): Stack<User> {
  let s = new Stack<User>();
  s.push(new User { name: "Ada", age: 36 });
  s.push(new User { name: "Grace", age: 85 });
  return s;
}

describe("Stack", () => {
  test("pop is LIFO", () => {
    let s = twoUsers();
    expect(s.pop()).toBe(new User { name: "Grace", age: 86 });
    expect(s.size()).toBe(1);
  });

  test("top borrows", () => {
    const s = twoUsers();
    expect(s.top()).not.toBeNull();
  });
});
```

`enrang test --ci src/stack.test.en`, stdout, exit status 1:

```text
FAIL src/stack.test.en
  Stack > pop is LIFO  FAILED
  Stack > top borrows

fail[T0001]: `toBe` found different values
  --> src/stack.test.en:11:12
   |
11 |     expect(s.pop()).toBe(new User { name: "Grace", age: 86 });
   |            ^^^^^^^ actual
   |                          ----------------------------------- expected
   |
   = note: difference at `.age`:
             User {
               name: 'Grace',
           -   age: 86,   expected
           +   age: 85,   actual
             }
   = note: `s.pop()` has type `User | null`; the non-null payload was compared.
   = note: ran on vm64, used 1204 of 200000000 fuel
help: rerun only this test
   |
   | enrang test src/stack.test.en -t "Stack > pop is LIFO"
   = explain: run `enrang explain T0001`

Tests  1 failed, 1 passed, 2 total
```

# Part II: the compiler's own tests

## 10. Principles and testkit

- **Written alongside each component from M0.** Tests are a condition for finishing a milestone (section 23). Test code and corpus are expected to meet or exceed implementation size.
- **`cargo test` is the single CI entry.** It calls `enrang test --reporter=json lib/` as a subprocess from M4. No third-party test crates.
- **M0-M3** run entirely under `cargo test` on testkit fixtures.
- **Stays in cargo:** compiler layers, encoder oracles, writers, matrix driver, meta-tests, runner self-tests.
- **Moves to `.test.en` after M4:** `core`, `alloc` and `os` library tests.
- **Conformance programs** stay plain, output-checked `.en` files that never depend on `expect`, so they validate new backends at M5 before native `enrang test` is trusted.
- **A broken runner cannot vouch for itself.** The must-fail suite gives each matcher and T code a test that has to fail with exact output.

`enrang-testkit` is a workspace crate used with `harness = false` test binaries, because libtest cannot generate tests dynamically.

```rust
pub struct SplitMix64(u64);  pub struct Xoshiro256([u64; 4]);          // section 14
pub trait Rng { fn next_u64(&mut self) -> u64; fn below(&mut self, n: u64) -> u64; fn edge_u64(&mut self) -> u64; }
pub fn shrink<T: Shrink>(failing: T, still_fails: impl FnMut(&T) -> bool) -> T;
pub fn golden(suite: &str, name: &str, actual: &str) -> Result<(), GoldenDiff>;   // section 17
pub fn tool(name: &str) -> Option<ToolPath>;                                      // section 20
pub fn skip(tool: &str, tests: usize, reason: &str);
pub fn skip_oracle(tool: &str, tests: usize, reason: &str);                       // the `oracle tests` wording of section 20
pub mod oracle { pub fn gas_assemble(mode: AsMode, march: &str, lines: &[String]) -> GasResult;
                 pub fn objdump_text(path: &Path) -> Vec<DisasmLine>; pub fn readelf(path: &Path) -> ElfFacts; }
pub mod decode;     // test-only x86 decoder, shares no tables with enrang-x86
pub mod x86sim;     // section 19
pub mod stepper;    // ptrace single-stepper; the only unsafe Rust in the repository
```

## 11. Layers and target counts at M10

| Layer | Cases | Generated per full run | Independent check |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Diagnostics, explain | 300 | | Every fix-it applied and recompiled |
| Lexer | 500 | 200k byte mutations, no panics | Token re-print |
| Parser (700 recovery cases) | 2,000 | 20k programs: format, reparse, equal AST | Formatter round trip |
| Formatter | 300 | The parser's 20k programs, formatted twice | Second run changes nothing; comments keep their text and order |
| Types, narrowing, generics | 2,500 | 5k programs | Maranget against brute force |
| Moves, borrows | 2,000 negative + 2,000 fixed siblings | 20k mutants | VM fault detector; `rustc` |
| MIR, drops, mono | 1,000 goldens | | Verifier after every pass; `rustc` drop traces |
| VM | 1,500 | | Fuel determinism; every fault detector fires |
| Encoder | About 800 forms, 3 modes | 300k instances, 20 `-march` names | `as` bytes; `objdump` decode; own decoder |
| ELF, PE, flat, image | 400 | | Own parser; `readelf`; `objdump -p`; execution |
| Backends | 1,000 | 100k `i64`-pair, 2M soft-float vectors | Rust host, bit for bit |
| Runtime, in `.test.en` | 1,500 | 100k allocator operations | Shadow model |
| Runner, CLI | 700, with must-fail suite | | Exact output |
| Dev, reload | 400 scenarios | 2k state walks | Migrated heap passes a typed walk |
| Boot | 300 bench scenarios | | Bench; QEMU when present |

That is about 17,000 cases, mostly table rows and rule-by-shape expansions.

## 12. Conformance corpus and implementation matrix

**Corpus.** Plain `.en` programs with headers, growing from 300 at M4 to 500 at M5 to 1,500 at M10. S1-S8, S10 and S11 are included verbatim, and S9 runs on the freestanding cells.

```ts
//! stdout: file            // or inline; expected bytes in name.stdout
//! exit: 0
//! allocs: balanced        // or an exact count
//! panic: "index 3 is out of bounds for length 3" at 7:15    // message and location
//! differs: sizeOf<usize>  // any difference between cells must be declared here
//! cells: hosted           // all | hosted | floor-only | vm ...
//! max-memory: 16MB        // VM cells: passes --max-memory
//! min-memory: 16MB        // freestanding cells skip this program, by declaration, below that
```

**Each cell compares:** stdout bytes, exit code, panic message and location, and allocation count. Stack overflow is excluded by declaration: the VM panics, and hosted targets report what the OS delivers. So is the end of a loop that grows without limit, such as an index loop that pushes every turn, because overcommit can get a native process killed where the allocator would have failed: a program relying on `out of memory` declares `//! cells: vm` and `//! max-memory:`, and one that loops at constant size is tested through T0004. Allocation counts come from a hidden `--alloc-stats` build flag, which writes counts on the framed channel at exit. A printed address is outside the stdout comparison too: a raw pointer, an `fn` value and a closure print one as `0x` and lowercase hex digits with no padding (SPEC 4.4). It differs between native cells by load address and is a VM address on a VM cell (`DEV_RUNTIME.md`), so a program that prints one declares `//! differs: address`, and each cell's stdout is compared with those addresses masked. Masking finds only that form, so a program whose output depends on an address in any other way, as `p as usize` printed in decimal or `(p as usize) % 64 == 0` does, runs on one cell, declared with `//! cells:` (`MD-7`, section 18). An address that comes from a constant, `0xB8000 as *u16`, prints `0xb8000` on every cell, VM cells included, because making a pointer from an integer never faults, and needs no declaration.

**The matrix:**

| Cell | Target | `--cpu` | Float | Runs on this machine | From |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `vm64` | VM | | host | Yes | M4 |
| `vm32` | VM | | host | Yes | M4 |
| `vm32-soft` | VM | | soft | Yes | M8 |
| `lx64` | `x86_64-linux` | `x86-64` | `sse2` | Natively | M5 |
| `win64` | `x86_64-windows` | `x86-64` | `sse2` | WSL interop | M7 |
| `l32-i386` | `i386-linux` | `i386` | `soft` | IA32 emulation | M8 |
| `l32-i486` | `i386-linux` | `i486` | `soft` | IA32 emulation | M8 |
| `l32-i586` | `i386-linux` | `i586` | `soft` | IA32 emulation | M8 |
| `l32-i686` | `i386-linux` | `i686` | `soft` | IA32 emulation | M8 |
| `l32-i586-x87` | `i386-linux` | `i586` | `x87` | IA32 emulation | M8 |
| `l32-i686-x87` | `i386-linux` | `i686` | `x87` | IA32 emulation | M8 |
| `l32-sse2s` | `i386-linux` | `sse2` | `soft` | IA32 emulation | M8 |
| `l32-sse2` | `i386-linux` | `sse2` | `sse2` | IA32 emulation | M8 |
| `none-bench` | `i386-none`, `lib/conformance/none_shim` | `i386` | `soft` | `x86sim`, models `i386-nofpu` and `pentium` | M9 |
| `none-qemu` | `i386-none`, same shim | `i386` | `soft` | Skip until QEMU; lowest model `486` | M9 |
| `none-qemu-sse2` | `i386-none`, same shim | `sse2` | `sse2` | Skip until QEMU; model `core2duo` | M9 |
| `none64-qemu` | `x86_64-none` | `x86-64` | `sse2` | Skip until QEMU | M10 |
| `efi-ovmf` | `x86_64-none --format=efi` | `x86-64` | `sse2` | Skip until OVMF | M10 |

`lib/conformance/none_shim` provides `console` on COM1, the SPEC 9.1 panic format, an allocator from `BootInfo`, and exit through `isa-debug-exit`, so hosted corpus programs run unchanged on freestanding cells. These cells build with `--stack-size=1048576` and use 32 MB of bench and QEMU memory, so recursion or heavy allocation that passes on hosted cells does not produce a false differential. A program needing more declares `//! min-memory:`. The 16 KB stack and 640 KB limits are asserted for S9 only.

`none-qemu-sse2` is the only cell that executes the freestanding SSE path, so it adds a kernel test that runs float work under a PIT interrupt whose handler also uses floats, compared bit for bit. That exercises the CR0 and CR4 enable sequence, `ldmxcsr`, `fxsave` stack alignment in stubs, and XMM survival across an interrupt.

**Cross-cuts.** `--regalloc=none|linear|chaos:<seed>` and `--opt=0|1` on `lx64` and `l32-i386`: a sample at default scale, all programs at `ENRANG_SCALE=10`. Every alias in SPEC 10.1 gets one hello build plus a golden of the note it prints; aliases add no matrix cells because they resolve to a rung.

This host supports every level on the list except AMD's 3DNow!, which we never emit, so floor-level and higher-level 32-bit binaries all run natively here [measured]. **That proves correctness of the code. The tags and audit of section 15 prove it stays within the level. Neither proves behaviour on the real chips.**

## 13. Differential testing

| Compared | What a difference means |
|---|---|
| VM against every native cell | Bug in the VM or a backend |
| `vm32` against `l32-*` and `vm64` against `lx64` | `sizeOf` included: layout bug |
| `linear` against `none` against `chaos:<seed>` | Emitter or allocator bug |
| `--opt=0` against `--opt=1` | LIR simplify bug |
| All `--cpu` levels | Level-dependent lowering bug |
| `soft` against `sse2` and `x87` against Rust host | Soft-float or x87 bug (section 16) |
| `--allocator=enrang` against host allocator in VM | `FreeListHeap` bug |
| `lx64` against `win64` | OS layer bug |
| GenOwn accept and reject against `rustc` | Borrow checker bug, or a named intended disagreement |
| Drop trace against `rustc` twin | Drop elaboration bug |

**The translation fixes the loop form.** A `for...of` over `&q` becomes `for x in &q`, or `for x in &mut q` when `x` is written through, in the body or after it through a binding that keeps it (`INF-09`). Changing `q` in the body is an error in Enrang too, so a growing loop no longer needs the index `while` loop that once kept the twins from disagreeing. A source that is already a reference and cannot be repointed, a parameter not marked `let`, a `const` reference local, or a call result or another source evaluated once, becomes `for x in src.iter()`, or `for x in src.iter_mut()` when `x` is written through (`INF-09`, `S2-25-pair`). A reference slot that can be repointed, a `let` local, a `let` parameter or a reference field, becomes `for x in r.iter()` or `r.iter_mut()` when the body never assigns `r`, and `let s = &r; for x in s.iter()`, or `let s = &mut r; for x in s.iter_mut()`, when it does, so `rustc` rejects assigning `r` in the body (E0506) as Enrang does (EN0706). The saved reference read by index at each head that these twins once used is rejected by `rustc` for `findLast` and `pairBig`, which take each turn's element from that one saved `&mut` (E0499 `` `s[_]` was mutably borrowed here in the previous iteration of the loop ``, and E0502), so it would report decided programs as borrow-checker bugs. The one shape on which the new form still splits from Enrang, `r = other; break;` in the body while a binding kept from the loop is used after it, which Enrang accepts and the twin rejects with E0506, is a named disagreement (`TK-02-reassign-break`). An owned source with no `&` keeps `for x in q`. `[...a]` becomes `out.extend(a.iter().cloned())`, in order. A reference variable becomes a new Rust binding at each assignment, because Enrang infers a mode per loan, not per binding. A function with a writable result gets two twins, `get` returning `&T` and `get_mut` returning `&mut T`, and each call uses the one its result's use demands; a `readonly` result gets only the first. `&T[]` becomes `&Vec<T>` or `&mut Vec<T>`, and `&Slice<T>` becomes `&[T]` or `&mut [T]`, by the inferred permission. `xs.slice(a, b)` and `xs.slice(a)` become a call to a twin helper that applies SPEC 8.2's index rule before slicing, since a Rust range panics where Enrang clamps (`SL-11-bounds`). A method that can be overridden becomes a trait method whose result shares one lifetime with `self` and with its base body's sources (SPEC 5.4), which a call through the method table uses: through `&C` or `Box<C>` of a class that isn't `final`, or `this.m()` inside one. A type parameter bounded by such a class, `T extends C`, becomes one bounded by `C`'s trait, so a call on a `T` place uses the trait method too (`D8-bound`). A direct call, on any receiver place of the class type that is not reached by dereferencing a `&C` or `Box<C>` (a local, a parameter, a field, an element, a tuple part, a case field, a narrowed payload or a temporary), through `super`, to a `private` method, to a method of a `final` class or to a `static` method, uses an inherent twin with its body's own lifetimes, which for a borrowing class follows the rule for reference fields in the next sentence. A borrowing class becomes a struct with two lifetimes (SPEC 5.8), `'r` for its reference fields declared `readonly &`, which are `&'r T`, and `'a` for its other reference fields, which are `&'a mut T` when the class writes through its reference fields (SPEC 5.1) and `&'a T` otherwise, and a borrowing tuple a tuple with one lifetime `'a`; a parameter it stores takes the lifetime of the field it is stored into, a method that stores into a `readonly &` field a reference reached from another reference field takes `where 'a: 'r`, a method that stores a reference reached from a `readonly &` field into a reference field not declared `readonly &` takes `where 'r: 'a`, which only a class whose flag is off can do, since with the flag on rule (e) reports the mark, EN0616 (`FV-02-swap`), and a method's result reached through those fields returns `&'r T` when it reaches the value only through `readonly &` fields and has no other source, and `&'x T` for a fresh `'x` with `where 'r: 'x` when it has other sources too, each of which takes `'x`, a result that holds, for `this`, the loans of the value's read set, never the object (SPEC 5.1, `FV-02-copier`, `FV-02-pick`), returns `&'a T` when it is read-only and the class does not write through its reference fields, with `where 'r: 'a` when a `readonly &` field is among its sources, and is tied to `self` otherwise.

**The translation also fixes drop order.** Enrang cleans up a value's fields and an enum case's fields in reverse declaration order and an array's elements from the last index down, and a derived class's destructor and fields before its base's, as C++ does, although a subclass's fields are created before its base's (SPEC 5.7 D2, `C4-drop-order`), where Rust drops fields in declaration order and elements in index order. So a twin struct or enum case declares its fields in reverse order, a subclass becomes a struct whose own fields, reversed, come before a last `base` field holding its base class's twin, and an array of values with destructors becomes a wrapper whose `Drop` drops the elements from the last index down. With these, `rustc` agrees on acceptance, on drop order, on EN0706 and on the EN0611 cases.

**Named intended disagreements** with `rustc`: NLL problem case #3, which Enrang accepts; evaluation-order patterns from O6, among them calls in an assignment target, which run before the right side, and a compound assignment's old value, which is read first when the operand is Copy (`F12`, `OQ25-order`, `S2-22-copy`); Enrang's own R1 rejections, which `rustc` accepts; bodies that form a writable result with one reference part, from a parameter the body does not also store, while a second reference to the same parameter is live, which Enrang accepts because such a result needs no exclusive-access proof in the callee (SPEC 5.4), and whose `get_mut` twin `rustc` rejects; `r = other; break;` in a loop over a reference slot `r` that can be repointed, while a binding kept from the loop is used after it, which Enrang accepts, since the `break` ends the hold, and whose twin is E0506 (`TK-02-reassign-break`); a compound assignment to an array element whose right side reads the array, `names[0] += pad(names.length())`, which Enrang accepts, as JavaScript does, and `rustc` rejects with E0502, because its index borrow is not two-phase (`SLB-02-element`); and two pairs of references from two turns of one loop that `rustc` accepts and Enrang makes read-only, a pair formed after the loop, which would need a path-by-path test after it (`TK-03-first-two`), and the last two matches kept across turns, which would need the order of earlier turns (`TK-03-last-two`), each EN0616 at the write with `` help: return the pair inside the loop ``; two fields of one turn's loop binding returned as a writable pair, `[&p.a, &p.b]`, or the parts `[k, v]` of a destructured binding, which `rustc` writes through and Enrang makes read-only, since both parts hold the binding's loan of this turn (`TK-03-fields-of-turn`), and two fields reached through one local reference, `const r = &p.inner; return [&r.a, &r.b];`, read-only for the same reason (`TK-01-local-ref`), each EN0614 at a push through a part; and a store into a `readonly &` field of a local borrowing value that a call returned, or of a by-value parameter, of a reference that holds a loan the value holds, as `const q = w.src; w.src = q;`, with EN0709 at a later move of `w` as well, since the copy takes a loan through `w` (`return w` in `FV-02-alias`), or as `const p = w.dst; w.src = p;` when nothing moves `w` or writes through `dst` afterwards, or into such a field of a value reset through a by-value parameter, as `c.reset(Copier.make(&tbl, &buf)); const p = c.dst!; c.src = p;`, which `rustc` accepts and Enrang rejects with EN0616, since no contract says which of such a value's loans its other fields hold, so it counts them all as held there, and the check rejects the store itself, where `rustc` rejects only a later write through the other field or a move (`FV-02-alias`, `FV-02-alias-peek`, `FV-02-alias-split`, `FV-02-reset`), and an inherited setter called on such a value with a token of its own read set, `w.retarget(w.peek())`, since the check keys on the value's own class (`FV-02-upcast-local`); a reference copied out of a reference field of `this` and kept across a call on the same object inside its class's own bodies, as `const p = this.src; this.step(); console.log(p);`, which `rustc` accepts for a field it types as a shared reference, since its borrow of the object does not reach through a shared reference's deref, and Enrang rejects with EN0706, since the copy holds a loan through the object, as a part that an auto-borrow takes through the field already does (`FV-02-field-copy`); a token read through a local reference to a borrowing value, from a reference field not declared `readonly &`, and kept across a call that changes the value, as `const r = &lx; const t = r.src.slice(0, 1); lx.setSrc(&other);` inside `Lexer`'s own body, which `rustc` accepts for a field it types as a shared reference and Enrang rejects with EN0706, since such a token keeps the reference's own loan on the value, as a borrow through any reference does (`FV-02-through-ref`); a call that stores into a value holding a loan of a consumed `this` or of a by-value borrowing parameter, the value itself, as `lx.setSrc(s)` on a `let lx: Lexer` parameter, one reached through it, or a local built from what it holds, as `c.retarget(s)` after `let c = new Copier(s, w.dst);` on a by-value `w: Copier`, which `rustc` accepts and Enrang rejects with EN0616, since nothing carries such a store to the caller's value and the checker cannot tell the value from what its fields reach, nor a value built from its fields from one reached through them (`FV-02-lost-store`); a method that narrows its own `readonly &` field from itself, called through a reference to the value that a call returned, `const r = id(&c); r.skip(); c.step();`, which `rustc` accepts and Enrang rejects with EN0707 at `c.step()`, since such a reference counts the loan of `&c` in its read set, so the store gives `c` a loan on itself, or called in a body that reaches the value through a parameter's reference field, `w.cp.skip()` in a `Wrapper.nudge(w: &Wrapper)`, which `rustc` accepts and Enrang rejects with EN0616 at `Wrapper.nudge(&w)`, since that body's own store then takes what all of `w`'s fields carry, with the read bit, so the read-set check finds the loans of `c` in `w`'s field written through (`FV-02-skip`); a `move` method that narrows its own `readonly &` field from itself and then moves `this` out, `this.src = this.src.slice(1, n); return this;`, which `rustc` accepts and Enrang rejects with EN0709 at `return this`, since the narrowed reference keeps the loan its slice took through `this`, which the move meets (SPEC 5.8 H05), while one that narrows the field and does not move `this` compiles (`FV-02-move`); a borrowing tuple copied out whole through a local reference to it, `const r = &pair; keep = *r;`, which `rustc` copies with no borrow of `pair` and Enrang rejects with EN0711 where `pair` is dropped while `keep` is still used, since such a copy keeps the reference's own loan on the value, as a borrow through any reference does (SPEC O9, `D20-core-3`, `FV-02-through-ref`); and a token read from a captured borrowing value's `readonly &` field inside an arrow and kept across a call that writes the value, `run(() => { const b = c.peek(); c.step(); console.log(b); })`, which `rustc` accepts and Enrang rejects with EN0706, since a read through `_env` holds the capture's loan (`FV-02-closure`). A result with several reference parts, or one whose source the body also stores, gets the proof or falls to read-only, so it is no disagreement (`INF-01`, `INF-02` in section 18). Nor is a compound assignment to a variable that is not Copy, which both reserve across the right side, so a right side that only reads it compiles in both and one that changes it fails in both (`S2-22-string`, `SLB-02-read`), or a pair of references bound in different turns of one run of a `for...of` loop and formed inside its body, over a parameter, an owned path, a narrowed path, or a view or copy of one, or over the binding of an outer loop over one, which both write through (`S2-25-pair`, `TK-03-tail`, `TK-03-copied-ref`, `TK-03-inner-pair`), or two owned fields of a borrowing class, or an owned field beside a part reached through a reference field, returned as a pair, which both write through, since a reference taken through `this` to a place that holds no reference holds nothing of what the reference fields carry (`TK-01-owned-fields`, `TK-01-own-view`), or a method that narrows its own `readonly &` field in a class whose flag is set, which both compile and call, on a local or through a reference parameter, a value that a call returned included (`FV-02-skip`), or a whole value written through a reference and then given a token of its own read set, which both accept (`FV-02-whole-write`). Each is one row in `corpus/rustc_disagreements.txt`, and a stale row fails. `rustc` is available here but its use is nightly-scale.

**Limit.** VM and native code share MIR, so they can agree on a wrong answer. Expected outputs and VM fault detection catch those, not the differential.

## 14. Generators

All generators are seeded, deterministic and written with our own PRNG. A failure prints its seed, shrinks, and lands in `corpus/regress/` as a permanent test. `ENRANG_SEED=<n>` reproduces.

**PRNG.** SplitMix64 seeds xoshiro256**. Known answers are pinned as a test [measured against a reference computation]:

```text
SplitMix64(seed 0):              0xE220A8397B1DCDAF  0x6E789E6AA1B965F4  0x06C45D188009454F
xoshiro256** seeded from above:  0x99EC5F36CB75F2B4  0xBF6E1F784956452A  0x1A5F849D4933E6E0
```

| Generator | Produces | Asserts |
|---|---|---|
| `GenBytes` | Random and mutated bytes | Lexer and parser never panic |
| `GenSyntax` | Valid ASTs | Format, reparse, equal AST; a second format changes nothing |
| `GenTypes` | Typed programs and near-misses | Accept or the intended code; no panic |
| `GenExpr` | Typed arithmetic whose expected value the generator computes itself, with float-to-int edges forced at `2^31`, `2^32`, `2^63`, `2^64` and one ulp below each | Exact output on every cell |
| `GenOwn` | Classes, moves, borrows and destructor tracers, with a Rust twin by the translation of section 13, including a `get` and a `get_mut` twin for each function with a writable result, borrowing classes and tuples that store, return and write through their reference fields, and `for...of` bodies that change the source, which must be rejected unless the loop is left first | `rustc` accept/reject and drop order; the VM's fault detector stays clean |
| `Mutants` | One violation inserted into an accepted program | Rejected with the intended code |
| Soundness | Every accepted program from the above | Runs fault-free under the VM; any fault is a soundness bug |
| `GenInstr` | Encoder instances: all registers, displacement sizes, immediate boundaries | `as` agreement |
| `GenFloat` | Edge-weighted float operands | Section 16 |
| `GenPair` | `i64` operations on edge values | i386 pairs equal Rust host |
| `GenSlice` | `slice(a, b)` and `slice(a)` on arrays, views and strings, with `a`, `b` and the length weighted towards 0, the length, one past it, negatives, `isize.MIN` and `isize.MAX` | The view lies inside its source, its start at most its end and its end at most the length, and equals the generator's own computation of ECMAScript's `slice` rule; no fault detector fires (`SL-11-bounds`). The strings include multi-byte characters: no string `slice` or `splitAt` panics, wherever its clamped indexes fall, and each clamped index that falls inside a character moves back to the start of that character, so every part starts and ends on a character boundary and equals the generator's own computation of that rule (`FV-06-char`, `FV-06-slice`) |
| `GenAlloc` | Allocator operation sequences, including sizes at the `isize.MAX` capacity boundary | `FreeListHeap` equals a shadow model; no overlap; `capacity overflow` exactly at the boundary |
| `GenSchema` | Random schema edits over random heaps, weighted towards zero-sized types and empty containers | Refused with unchanged heap hash, or migrated with a clean census (`DEV_RUNTIME.md` section 16) |

**Scale.** `ENRANG_SCALE=1` is the default, a tenth of the full volume. `ENRANG_SCALE=10` is the full volume, and nightly uses 100.

## 15. Encoder oracle and level enforcement

### 15.1 Bytes

Every form, in every valid mode, runs through all registers, displacement sizes and immediate boundaries. `as` with `.intel_syntax noprefix` and `.code16/32/64` assembles each one, and the bytes are compared. One file per mode and one `as` run; the listing gives bytes per line. `as` assembled 200,000 lines in 0.13 s, or 0.55 s with a byte listing [measured], so batching makes this cheap.

- **Alternate encodings.** `{load}`, `{store}`, `{disp8}` and `{disp32}` at line start force the choice [measured: `{load} mov eax, ebx` is `8B C3`, `{store} mov eax, ebx` is `89 D8`, `{disp32} mov eax, [ebx+4]` is `8B 83 04 00 00 00`]. Where GAS has a default, the encoder uses the same one.
- **Prefix order** must match GAS: segment, `67`, `66`, `rep` or `lock`, mandatory prefix, REX. In `.code16`, `addr32 rep movsd` is `67 66 F3 A5`, `mov byte ptr es:[ebx], 1` is `26 67 C6 03 01`, and `lock add dword ptr fs:[eax], 1` is `64 67 66 F0 83 00 01` [measured].
- **Second decoder.** `objdump -D -b binary -m i8086` confirms the 16-bit forms decode as intended [measured for `66 EA`, `66 0F 01 16`, `0F 20 C0`], and the test-only decoder in `enrang-testkit` checks round trips with no shared tables.

### 15.2 Level tags against the assembler

The same files run per `-march`. For each form `f` and level `L`: `allowed(L) ⊇ f.features` must equal "`as -march=map(L)` accepts", except for rows in `tests/oracle_exceptions.txt`.

| Level | `as` name | Mode |
|---|---|---|
| `i386` | `i386`; x87 probe forms at `i386+387` | `--32` |
| `i486` | `i486`; x87 probe forms at `i486+387` | `--32` |
| `i586` | `i586` | `--32` |
| `i686` | `i686` | `--32` |
| `sse2` | `pentium4` | `--32` |
| `x86-64` | `generic64` | `--64` |
| Stage 1 (allowed set lacking `Ia32`) | `.code16` then `.arch i8086`, in that order | `--32` |

The stage 1 row has a real oracle. With `.code16` before `.arch i8086`, `as` accepts `mov ax, bx`, `int 0x10`, `div bx`, `pushf` and `shl ax, 1`, and rejects `push 1`, `shl ax, 4`, `pusha`, `mov eax, ebx`, `movzx` and `lgdt`. A long `jc` assembles to `73 03 E9 ...` where `i386` gives `0F 82 ...` [measured]. The reverse directive order is an error [measured], which is what made this look impossible. The whole of stage 1 runs through this row, byte and level.

`generic64` was chosen because it rejects `cmpxchg16b`, `lahf`, `sahf` and `popcnt` [measured], which is exactly the baseline's boundary. Named-chip cross-checks also run, per form, at `pentium`, `pentiumpro`, `pentiumii`, `pentiumiii`, `prescott`, `nocona`, `core`, `core2`, `corei7`, `k6`, `k6_2`, `athlon`, `opteron`, `k8`, `generic32`.

**Harness rules** that keep a typo from reading as a rejection:

- A canary `nop` at each `-march` proves the CPU name is valid. A bad name is `Fatal error: invalid -march= option` [measured], and there is no `penryn`.
- A level rejection is exactly one of two messages [measured]: `` `bswap' is not supported on `i386' `` or `` unsupported instruction `nop' ``.
- Any other error, such as `operand size mismatch`, fails the harness itself.

**Measured acceptance, `as --32 -march=X`, `as` 2.42** (Y accepted, `.` rejected):

```text
            bswap cmpxchg xadd cpuid rdtsc cx8b cmov fcomi fninit ud2 movcr4 nopl fxsave addsd addss mmx femms invlpg wrmsr
i386          .      .     .     .     .    .    .     .     .     Y    Y     .     .     .     .    .    .     .     .
i386+387      .      .     .     .     .    .    .     .     Y     Y    Y     .     .     .     .    .    .     .     .
i486          Y      Y     Y     Y     .    .    .     .     .     Y    Y     .     .     .     .    .    .     Y     .
i486+387      Y      Y     Y     Y     .    .    .     .     Y     Y    Y     .     .     .     .    .    .     Y     .
i586          Y      Y     Y     Y     Y    Y    .     .     Y     Y    Y     .     .     .     .    .    .     Y     Y
i686          Y      Y     Y     Y     Y    Y    Y     Y     Y     Y    Y     .     Y     .     .    .    .     Y     Y
pentiumpro    Y      Y     Y     Y     Y    Y    Y     Y     Y     Y    Y     Y     Y     .     .    .    .     Y     Y
pentiumii     Y      Y     Y     Y     Y    Y    Y     Y     Y     Y    Y     Y     Y     .     .    Y    .     Y     Y
pentiumiii    Y      Y     Y     Y     Y    Y    Y     Y     Y     Y    Y     Y     Y     .     Y    Y    .     Y     Y
pentium4      Y      Y     Y     Y     Y    Y    Y     Y     Y     Y    Y     Y     Y     Y     Y    Y    .     Y     Y
k6            Y      Y     Y     Y     Y    Y    .     .     Y     Y    Y     .     .     .     .    Y    .     Y     Y
k6_2          Y      Y     Y     Y     Y    Y    .     .     Y     Y    Y     .     .     .     .    Y    Y     Y     Y
athlon        Y      Y     Y     Y     Y    Y    .     Y     Y     Y    Y     Y     .     .     .    Y    Y     Y     Y
```

64-bit, `as --64` [measured]: `cmpxchg16b` rejected at `generic64` and `k8`, accepted at `nocona`, `core2`, `corei7`. `lahf` and `sahf` rejected at `generic64`, `nocona`, `k8`, `core2`, accepted at `corei7`. `popcnt` accepted only at `corei7`. `pshufb` accepted at `core2`, `corei7`. `pmulld` accepted only at `corei7`. `nop dword ptr [rax]`, `cvttsd2si rax, xmm0` and `fxsave` accepted at all five.

Floor forms used by the compiler, accepted at `i386` [measured]: `lock bts`, `lock inc`, `xchg`, `lock or dword ptr [esp], 0`, `rep movsd`, `shld`, `movzx`, `sete`, `bt`, three-operand `imul`, `cdq`, `jo`, `leave`, `iretd`, `lgdt`, `mov cr0, eax`. `popad` and `enter` are accepted by `as` but banned by us.

### 15.3 Recorded disagreements

Our feature table is the source of truth. `tests/oracle_exceptions.txt` holds one row per disagreement: instruction, `-march`, what `as` says, what we say, reason. **A stale row fails the test.** Seeded with:

| `as` behaviour [measured] | Our table | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| `athlon` rejects `cmov` | K7 is at `i686`, which has CMOV | Real K7 chips have it |
| `athlon` rejects `fxsave` | `fxsave` needs `sse2` level | K7 model 2+ has it, K7 model 1 lacks it; we never rely on it below `sse2` |
| `athlon` accepts `nop dword ptr` | NOPL only at `x86-64` | No K7 source found |
| `i486` accepts `cpuid` | `cpuid` needs `i586` | Early 486s lack it |
| `i386`, `i486`, `i586` accept `ud2` | `ud2` tagged P6 (`i686`) | Generated code uses neither; panics use `int3` |
| `i386`, `i486` accept `mov cr4, eax` | `cr4` needs `i586` | A 386 has no CR4 |
| `i686` rejects `nop dword ptr`; `pentiumpro` through `pentium4` accept | NOPL in no 32-bit level | Avoids the question; `x86-64` only |
| `pentiumiii` accepts `addss` and `ldmxcsr`, `i686` rejects them | Tagged `sse`; only level `sse2` has them | `sse` alone is never a rung, because it cannot do `f64` |
| `i686`, `pentiumpro`, `pentiumii` accept `fxsave` | `fxsave` needs `sse2` | Pentium Pro and Klamath lack it |
| `i386`, `i486` reject `fninit`, `i586` accepts | `fpu` is in no 32-bit level; user `asm` reaches only the probe forms | `+387` names used for the probe audit |
| `nocona`, `core2` accept `cmpxchg16b` | Not in baseline | Early 64-bit parts lack it |
| `core2` rejects long-mode `lahf` | Not in baseline | Real Core 2 has it; we never emit it |

### 15.4 Enforcement tests

1. **Refusal.** `Assembler::emit` refuses each form at each level below its tag. In generated code that is an internal error; in inline assembly, EN0901.
2. **Inline diagnostics.** EN0901 per feature names the instruction, the feature, the level providing it and the current level. G15 is the byte-exact golden.
3. **Output audit.** Tags could share the emitter's mistakes, so we disassemble emitted `.text` with `objdump -d`, keep numeric branch targets, reassemble at `-march=<level>`, and require every line accepted. This was tried: it flagged only `bswap` at `i386` [measured]. Dropping branch targets gives false failures. It runs on the corpus at every level, under these rules:
   - **Audit `--symbols` builds.** Default ELF output has `e_shnum = 0`, and `objdump -d` on an ELF32 with no section headers prints no instructions and exits 0 [measured], so auditing it would pass vacuously. The test asserts that every `PT_LOAD` byte of the `--symbols` build equals the default build.
   - **No vacuous pass.** Decoded instruction bytes not equal to the text size, or zero lines, is a harness failure.
   - **Flat and images** use `objdump -D -b binary -m i386` with start and stop addresses from `<out>.map`, excluding the BPB, the parameter block and `db` ranges.
   - **Negative control.** A fixture with `bswap` injected at level `i386` must fail the audit.
4. **Independent scan.** The test-only decoder classifies every instruction in `.text` against a feature table written separately from the encoder's.
5. **Dynamic classification.** A ptrace single-stepper classifies every executed opcode across a sample of the i386-linux corpus. Feasibility [measured]: a 64-bit tracer single-stepped a 32-bit ELF here through `PTRACE_SINGLESTEP`, with the i386 regset (68 bytes), in a 7-step run. About 300 lines in `enrang-testkit`, using raw syscalls.
6. **Floor island.** Assembler records and the audit prove startup, level check and panic printing stay at the floor at every `--cpu`, with FPU probe forms audited at `i386+387`.
7. **Failing cleanly.** `checkLevel(flags)` is table-driven and tested with fake flags for every family. The real freestanding branches cannot run on this CPU, so the bench runs them (section 19). The hosted path runs end to end under the ptrace stepper with a CPU personality: after `pushfd` it clears AC or ID in the pushed image, and at `cpuid` it skips the instruction and sets registers from the family table. The real ELF32 must then print the golden message and exit 126, for each rung and family, with no emulator.
8. **Baseline.** The `x86-64` audit at `generic64` rejects CMPXCHG16B, LAHF, SAHF, POPCNT.
9. **Errata.** Compiled floor output contains no `popa`, `popad`, `enter`, `ud2` or `loop`; the decoder asserts this directly, since `as` accepts most of them.

## 16. Soft-float against hardware

**Oracle.** Rust `f32`/`f64` on this host compile to SSE2 scalar instructions, so test code computes bit-exact expected results with the real hardware. Plain Rust operators serve only for non-NaN results, because LLVM treats `fadd` and `fmul` as commutative and may swap operands, which changes which NaN propagates. For the stricter NaN test, `enrang-testkit`, the only crate allowed `unsafe`, uses `core::arch::asm!("addsd {a}, {b}")` per operation, so operand order is fixed and a `rustc` upgrade cannot fail correct soft-float code.

**Driver.** `corpus/softfloat/driver.en` reads binary vectors (`op`, operands) from stdin and writes result bits to stdout. The Rust harness generates vectors with `GenFloat` and compares.

**Implementations compared, bit for bit:**

```text
Rust host SSE2 = l32-i386 soft = vm32-soft = l32-sse2 = lx64 = win64 = l32-i586-x87 = l32-i686-x87
```

- **Language guarantee** (SPEC 9.3): non-NaN results bit-identical, NaN-ness identical, NaNs canonicalised by `toBits`.
- **Internal test, stricter:** soft-float also matches SSE2 NaN sign and payload for add, sub, mul, div, sqrt.

**Vector classes**, for both widths, weighted to edges:

| Class | Examples |
|---|---|
| Specials | `±0`, `±inf`, qNaN, sNaN, every pairing |
| Subnormals | Boundary to normal, min subnormal, results that underflow exactly at the rounding boundary |
| Rounding | Exact halfway cases, one ulp either side, ties to even in both directions |
| Exponent | Maximum differences, overflow by one, cancellation to zero and to subnormal |
| Random | Uniform bits, uniform exponents |

**Operations:** `+ - * / %`, `sqrt`, compare with all predicates, `f32`-`f64` conversions, `i32`, `u32`, `i64`, `u64` to float and back (range-checked, since `as` panics; Rust's saturating `as` is filtered), `floor`, `ceil`, `trunc`, `round`, `abs`, `min`, `max`, `fromBits`, `toBits`. `%` compares against Rust's exact `%`.

**Printing and parsing.** Dragon4 output is compared digit-for-digit against Rust `{:e}`, then the JS-style formatting rules separately; parsing against Rust `parse`. SPEC S1's `0.30000000000000004` is a golden.

**Volume.** 200k vectors by default, 2M at scale 10, 20M nightly. M8 records real soft-float instruction counts with the stepper, replacing the clock estimates in SPEC 9.3 for a documented table.

**x87 cells from M8.** The x87 cells of section 12 run these same vectors on the host's own x87 through IA32 emulation, including double rounding on subnormals (SPEC 9.3). That proves the code, not old FPUs. An x87 build that divides also links the software division and remainder that a Pentium with the FDIV flaw uses, counted in the soft-float budget below. Freestanding x87 runs need QEMU, or x87 arithmetic in `x86sim`, which today decodes only the probe forms.

**Budget.** Soft-float code, if referenced, is at most 16 KB, and the audit runs it at `i386`.

## 17. Goldens and bless

- `tests/golden/<suite>/<name>` holds rendered output. `ENRANG_BLESS=1 cargo test` rewrites and prints diffs, and refuses under `CI=1`. Orphaned goldens fail.
- `//~` anchors are hand-written and cannot be blessed, so blessing never changes which error is expected.
- **SPEC is the golden source for its own examples.** A test extracts every G and S case, the tour, the tour's kernel and the `Ownership in three lines` block from `docs/SPEC.md` and compares compile, stderr, stdout, serial output and exit status byte for byte. If a spec example and a golden disagree, the test fails, so neither can drift. Extraction reads only the `<!-- case: ... -->` markers and the blocks after them (SPEC 14), never prose, and the same test extracts section 9 of this document, which gets its marker at M4, when that case is first enforced. Each case is enforced from the milestone its codes and features reach, by the `since` rule of section 18.
- **`enrang fmt`**, from M1, is tested four ways: the tree is the same after formatting, apart from the order of import lines and of the names in each import list, which fmt sorts (SPEC 12.5); a second run changes nothing; every comment keeps its text and order, apart from the leading spaces of its first line and of `*` lines, and trailing spaces it removes; and every code block of Enrang source in SPEC is already formatted, except G01-G04, which are invalid on purpose (examples quoted inline in SPEC prose may stay on one line and are not checked). The parser's format-and-reparse round trips in sections 11 and 14 compare trees the same way.
- **Quotes and import order** have fixed cases, each passing the four tests above. Quotes (`EF-05`): `'He said "hi" and it\'s, isn\'t'`, a tie, becomes `"He said \"hi\" and it's, isn't"`; `"a\"b\"c'd"` becomes `'a"b"c\'d'`; `'a"b\'\''` becomes `"a\"b''"`. The chosen quote is escaped wherever the text holds it, and Prettier 3.9.4 gives the same text for the first and third. Imports (`EF-11`): the group `import { b } from 'a';`, `import { a } from "b";`, `public import { Z } from "./z";`, `import * as core from "core";` sorts by each path's value read after the Quotes rule, so `public import { Z } from "./z";` comes first, then `"a"`, `"b"` and `"core"`; a `public import` sorts with the other lines, and two lines with one path keep their order. A key taken from the source spelling would put `"b"` before `'a'` and fail the second-run test.
- Goldens never contain timings, absolute paths or memory addresses. `--ci` removes them; the renderer has no other source of nondeterminism.

## 18. Traceability meta-tests

`define_*!` macros generate each enum plus an `ALL` list, since Rust has no reflection. **The build fails if any row that has reached its milestone has a gap.**

**Milestone gating.** No compiler exists at M0, so an unconditional "every code has a fixture" would stay red until M10. Every macro row and extracted case carries `since(Mn)`: `define_codes!` (SPEC 13.2), `define_mir_statements!`, `define_intrinsics!`, encoder forms, lang items, and G and S cases. Meta-tests enforce only rows with `since <= CURRENT_MILESTONE`, a constant bumped by the commit that closes a milestone. A row whose `since` has not been reached must not yet have a fixture that claims to pass, so a stale `since` also fails. A case marker has no `since` field, so extracted cases take theirs from a side table, `tests/trace/case_since.txt`, added at M1 from section 23.

**Document row IDs.** Prose tables have no macro, so their rows carry stable IDs, and a retired ID is never reused (SPEC 4.5 retires C01-C03): `C04` (SPEC 4.5), `H01` (SPEC 5.8), `MIG-01`, `REF-01`, `FAIL-01`, `ADV-1` (`DEV_RUNTIME.md` sections 7, 8, 10, 12, the last of which has bare numbers in its `#` column). A test parses the IDs from the Markdown into `tests/trace/*.txt`, and fixtures declare `//! covers: MIG-07`. An uncovered ID and an orphan `covers` both fail, subject to `since`. So these gates are mechanical rather than a reviewer's judgement.

| Item | Required |
|---|---|
| Each `fixture(Compile)` code | A firing fixture, a passing near-miss, an explain page whose examples compile |
| Each `fixture(Cli)`, `fixture(Reload)` or `fixture(TestRun)` code | A firing scenario of that kind, an explain page with a transcript. EN1215 cannot be shown by a compile failure |
| Each MIR statement kind and intrinsic | Hit by probe counters in the VM and in every backend. `Drop` exists only before elaboration, so MIR goldens cover it; `HostCall` runs only in the VM, `Asm` only natively |
| Each encoder form | Byte oracle, level oracle, and one emission test or inline test |
| Each runtime function | Hit in VM block counters or, for `os/*` and asm-only code, in a native or bench cell. Exemption file must stay empty for `core` and `alloc` |
| `asm` placement | No `asm` in `core` outside `core/port` and `core/cpu`; from M4, the mono closure of S1-S8, S10 and S11 on VM rows contains no `Asm` statement |
| Floor island | No `Fp` LIR instruction in an island function |
| Stage 1 and M16 | The M16 form list equals the forms `lib/boot` assembles |
| Each `@lang` item | Exists in every target's module graph, plus a test |
| Each CLI command, flag, matcher and Jest hint | A golden |
| Each deferred feature, SPEC 16 | Fixture producing exactly its message head |
| Each VM fault detector | A test making it fire |
| Each `C`, `H`, `MIG`, `REF`, `FAIL` and `ADV` row | A fixture or scenario declaring `covers` |
| Each size budget | A failing check from its milestone |
| Each `G` and `S` heading in SPEC | A `case` marker |

**Named fixtures.** These programs reached a design review as concrete soundness or acceptance failures, so each is a permanent fixture, with a `rustc` twin where the row says so.

Covers ADV-54 |

**`for...of` and array spread.** Where no result is stated with `rustc`, the program was traced by hand through the loop's hold (SPEC 5.6), O9-O11 and `ARCHITECTURE.md` section 6.2. "Twin" means a `rustc` twin by the translation of section 13. A loop over `&q` holds `q`, and every reference slot or reference field its path goes through, while a next head of the same run is reachable, so changing or assigning `q` in the body is an error unless the loop is left first. Taking the hold at the pre-header defines it anew, so a run's hold is dead once the loop is left. Every turn binds at the loop's one bind site, whose loans the loop's own head and bind skip, so a binding kept from an earlier turn overlaps neither a later turn's bind nor the head's tag checks and `Len`, while another loop over `q`, or any other access to it, still overlaps it as `[*]` does. Compared with one another, as N6's part test compares the parts of a result, the bind site's loans carry one of three keys, this turn, earlier turns of this run and earlier runs: each bind renames this turn's key into earlier turns of this run, and each pre-header renames this turn's and earlier turns' keys into earlier runs, so the last binding of one run is never taken for an earlier turn of the next. Two of them are disjoint only when one is this turn's and the other an earlier turn's of the same run; this turn against itself, and anything against earlier runs, overlap (`S2-25-pair`).

| Fixture | Must give |
|---|---|
| The owner's form: `let queue = ["a", "b"]; for (const job of &queue) { queue.push("retry"); }` | EN0706, `cannot mutate `queue` while iterating over it` (twin E0502) |
| The same over `queue` with no `&` | EN0601: the loop takes `queue`, so the body cannot use it |
| `for (const job of [...queue]) { queue.push("retry"); }` | Compiles; 2 turns, 4 items |
| `for (const job of &queue) { queue.push("retry"); console.log(job); }` | EN0706 only (G08) |
| `queue.push(job.clone());` in the body | EN0706 only; O6 takes the receiver's auto-borrow after the argument |
| `const xs = []; xs.push(1);`; `for (const x of &xs) { *x = 1; }` on a `const` | EN0614; EN0608 at the source |
| `const r = &q; for (const x of r) { *x = 1; }` with `let q` | Compiles; the hold is exclusive, because `x` is written |
| In a loop over `&q`: `q.push(1)`, `const r = &q; r.push(1)`, `if (c) { q.push(1); }`, `q[0] = 9`, `q = []; continue;`; with source `&o.items`: `o.clear()`, `reset(&o)` with a `reset` that changes `o`, `o = make()`; `g(q)` in a loop over `q: &T[]`, with a `g` that pushes; `a = [0; 3]` in a loop over `&a` with `a: i64[3]` | EN0706 each |
| `run(() => { q.push(1); })` in a loop over `&q` | EN0706, `` cannot mutate `q` while iterating over it ``: the arrow's exclusive capture of `q` meets the loop's hold, which is tried first (`ARCHITECTURE.md` section 6.2) |
| In a loop over `&q`: `q.push(1); break;`, `q = []; break;`, `q.clear(); return;`, `q = []; return;`; `q = []` after the loop; `q.push(1)` in an index `for` loop; `for (const x of &xs) { *x = 1; }` with `let xs` | Compiles |
| `for (const a of &q) { for (const b of &q) { q.push(1); } }`; two pushes in one loop over `&queue` | One EN0706 (twin E0502); one EN0706 at each push (twin E0502 twice) |
| `const q = [1]; for (const x of &q) { q.push(2); }` | EN0614 and EN0706 (twin E0596 and E0502) |
| An unrelated EN0706 and warning EN0453 from `name.trim();` in one file | Both, ending `; 1 warning emitted` (G23) |
| Nested, outer element used after an inner push: `for (const a of &xs) { for (const b of &xs) { xs.push(1); } console.log(a); }` | EN0706 (twin E0502) |
| Inner source is the outer element: `for (const row of &grid) { for (const c of row) { grid.push([]); } }` | EN0706 (twin E0502). With `break` after the push: still EN0706, because the outer loop's next head is reachable (twin E0502). With `break outer`: compiles (twin accepts) |
| `LTK-4`, nested loops over `xs: &Slice<T>` that write their elements: `for (const a of xs) { for (const b of xs) { *b = 1; } *a = 2; }`, with `a` live at the inner element write; an outer written element live at an inner head that only reads | EN0707 at the inner loop's `xs`: another loop's hold, head or bind that meets a `Hold` reports by mode, since the `Hold`'s EN0706 in both columns is kept for accesses that change the held source (twin E0499); EN0708 |
| `saved = job; queue.push(s); use(saved);`, and `best = x` with `q.push` while `best` is live | EN0706 (twin E0502) |
| A `const` binding over many turns | No EN0613: each turn binds a new `x`, and `StorageDead(x)` ends the last one |
| `let prev: &i64 \| null = null; for (const x of &xs) { if (prev != null) { *prev = 9; } prev = x; }` over `[1, 2, 3]`; `let keep: &i64 \| null = null; for (const x of &q) { if (c) { keep = x; } } use(keep);` with a `use` that writes through its argument | Compile, and the first prints `[9, 9, 3]` (twins over `iter_mut` agree): every turn binds at the loop's one bind site, whose loans the loop's own head and bind skip, so a binding kept from an earlier turn overlaps neither the next bind nor the head's `Len`; `kill(x)` removes only loans rooted at `x` |
| `let keep: &i64 \| null = null; for (const x of &q) { keep = x; } use(keep);` with a `use` that writes `0` through its argument | Compiles, and over `q = [1, 5, 3, 7]` prints `[1, 5, 3, 0]` for `console.log(q)` (twin over `iter_mut` agrees): `keep` holds a loan of the loop's bind site, which only the loop's own head and bind skip; nothing touches `q` while `keep` is live, and `console.log(q)` runs after its last use (`TK-04`) |
| The loop of the row above run twice, `let n = 0; while (n < 2) { for (const x of &q) { keep = x; } n += 1; }`, then `use(keep);` with the same writing `use`; the same with a `use` that only reads | EN0707 at the inner loop's `&q`, once (twin E0499): a loop starts by taking its hold at the pre-header, a borrow of `q` checked against every live loan, and on the second run that meets the binding kept from the first, which the head's `Len` and the bind skip as the loop's own turn keys, while the first run's own hold is dead there, since taking the hold defines it anew (`S2-23-rerun`); compiles, since every loan is shared (twin over `iter` agrees). A named target of the second attack pass |
| `S2-23-rerun`: `let q: i64[] = [1, 2]; let n = 0; while (n < 2) { for (const x of &q) { *x += 1; } q.push(n); n += 1; } console.log(q);`; `while (n < 2) { for (const x of &q) { if (*x == 0) { q.push(1); break; } } n += 1; }` over `let q: i64[] = [0, 2];`, then `console.log(q)` | Compile and print `[3, 4, 1, 1]` and `[0, 2, 1, 1]` (twins agree): the hold is live only while a next head of the same run is reachable, and the next run's pre-header takes a new one, so neither `q.push(n)` after the inner loop nor that pre-header meets the previous run's hold |

A body that writes through the binding over `&TABLE` is `S2-14-const` |
